Our results suggest that LLMs can perform a range of data preparation tasks at scale with minimal human intervention, unlocking substantive research questions previously beyond practical reach, though implementation requires attention to costs, licensing, and other ethical considerations.

replace LLMs Can Get "Brain Rot": A Pilot Study on Twitter/X

Authors: Shuo Xing, Junyuan Hong, Yifan Wang, Runjin Chen, Zhenyu Zhang, Ananth Grama, Zhengzhong Tu, Zhangyang Wang

Abstract: We propose and test the LLM Brain Rot Hypothesis: continual exposure to junk web text induces lasting cognitive decline in large language models (LLMs). To unveil junk effects, we designed a novel controlled experiment on real Twitter/X corpora, by constructing junk and reverse-controlled datasets via two orthogonal operationalizations: M1 (engagement degree) and M2 (semantic quality), with matched token scale and training operations across conditions. Compared to the control group, continual pre-training of 4 LLMs on the junk dataset causes non-trivial declines (Hedges' g>0.3) on reasoning, long-context understanding, safety, and inflating "dark traits" (e.g., psychopathy, narcissism). The gradual mixtures of junk and control datasets also yield dose-response cognition decay: for example, under M1, ARC-Challenge with Chain-of-Thought drops 72.1 -> 57.2 and RULER-CWE 83.7 -> 52.3 as junk ratio rises from 0% to 100%. Error forensics reveal several key insights. First, we identify thought-skipping as the primary lesion in reasoning: models increasingly truncate or skip chains. Second, partial but incomplete healing is observed: scaling instruction tuning and clean continual pre-training improve the declined cognition, yet cannot restore baseline capability, suggesting persistent representational drift rather than format mismatch. Finally, we discover that the popularity, a non-semantic metric, of a tweet is a better indicator of the Brain Rot effect than the length in M1. Together, the results provide significant, multi-perspective evidence that social effects of data could be a causal driver of LLM capability decay in continual pre-training, thereby motivating routine "cognitive health checks" for deployed and evolving LLMs.

replace Reclaiming Low-Quality Instruction Data with Rule-Guided LLM Reconstruction

Authors: Zile Yang, Ling Li, Na Di, Jinlong Pang, Yao Zhou, Hao Cheng, Bo Han, Jiaheng Wei

Abstract: Data selection for supervised fine-tuning (SFT) often discards low-scoring instruction examples that may retain useful supervision. We propose a rule-guided LLM reconstruction framework to reclaim this information. The framework identifies low-scoring samples through LLM-based rating and score correction, selects semantically representative sources, and reconstructs instruction-response pairs through strategy-guided generation, criteria-based validation, and failure-guided repair. From 123,786 low-scoring samples, it constructs 54,888 training examples. Across three 7B/8B base LLMs, the reconstructed data achieves higher average downstream performance than the low-scoring source set and all evaluated data-selection baselines. Despite using substantially fewer examples than the full source dataset (approximately 300K), it achieves higher reported average scores than full-data training across all three base models. Stepwise ablations identify rule-guided reconstruction as the largest source of observed improvements over direct fusion, while a manual audit reveals remaining limitations in source integration and answer faithfulness. These findings support reconstruction as a complementary approach to selection-based SFT data curation.

replace QuantEval: A Benchmark for Financial Quantitative Tasks in Large Language Models

Authors: Zhaolu Kang, Junhao Gong, Wenqing Hu, Yingjie He, Kehan Jiang, Leqi Zheng, Shuo Yin, Zhicheng Fang, Chunlei Meng, Eric Hanchen Jiang, Yunfei Feng, Yitong Leng, Junfan Zhu, Xiaoyou Chen, Xi Yang, Richeng Xuan

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong capabilities in finance-oriented question answering, yet their evaluation on professional quantitative workflows remains fragmented and often stops at factual or document-grounded QA. We introduce QuantEval, a benchmark for evaluating whether LLMs can complete the compute--implement--validate loop required in quantitative finance. QuantEval contains 1,575 expert-validated instances across three complementary dimensions: (i) knowledge-based QA, (ii) quantitative mathematical reasoning, and (iii) quantitative strategy coding. The coding task is evaluated with a deterministic CTA-style backtesting framework that executes model-generated strategies and compares risk--return metrics against expert references, moving beyond syntax or template matching. We evaluate 13 open-source and proprietary LLMs, report prompt and reliability analyses, and observe substantial gaps relative to human experts, especially in multi-step reasoning and framework-constrained coding. We further conduct supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning experiments on domain-aligned data, showing that training can improve quantitative reasoning while also exposing trade-offs in factual recall. QuantEval releases the dataset, evaluation scripts, and full backtesting configuration to support reproducible research on LLMs for quantitative finance.

replace CALM-IT: Enhancing Synthetic Motivational Interviewing Dialogue Quality with Conversational Dynamics

Authors: Viet Cuong Nguyen, Nhi Yen Nguyen, Kristin A. Candan, Mary Conlon, Vanessa Rumie, Kristen Risola, Michael L. Birnbaum, Munmun De Choudhury

Abstract: Conversational dynamics describe how rapport, emotion, and goals change over a dialogue. These changes are important in therapeutic conversations, where they can influence when and how therapists intervene. However, large language model (LLM)-based synthetic therapeutic dialogue generators rarely track how these properties evolve together, which may limit the quality of longer conversations. We introduce CALM-IT, a dual-agent framework that tracks client states and therapist-side inferences to guide strategy selection and utterance generation. We evaluate synthetic Motivational Interviewing (MI) dialogues using automated Motivational Interviewing Treatment Integrity (MITI) ratings, conversational metrics, and comparisons across dialogue lengths, with ratings from four licensed psychologists providing support for key findings. Across 8,232 dialogues covering 686 grounded scenarios, four frameworks, and three target lengths, CALM-IT achieves the highest mean scores on all four MITI 4.2 global ratings and shows the smallest aggregate change in quality between short- and long-form conditions. CALM-IT also achieves the highest simulated client redirection acceptance rate (64.3\%), with its strongest redirections followed by increased change talk and reduced sustain talk. Ablation experiments show that removing conversational dynamics components reduces dialogue quality. Together, these results suggest that explicitly modeling conversational dynamics can improve the quality and consistency of long-form synthetic MI dialogues.

replace MasalBench: A Benchmark for Contextual and Cross-Cultural Understanding of Persian Proverbs in LLMs

Authors: Ghazal Kalhor, Behnam Bahrak

Abstract: In recent years, multilingual Large Language Models (LLMs) have become an inseparable part of daily life, making it crucial for them to master the rules of conversational language in order to communicate effectively with users. While previous work has evaluated LLMs' understanding of figurative language in high-resource languages, their performance in low-resource languages remains underexplored. In this paper, we introduce MasalBench, a comprehensive benchmark for assessing LLMs' contextual and cross-cultural understanding of Persian proverbs, which are a key component of conversation in this low-resource language. We evaluate eight state-of-the-art LLMs on MasalBench and find that they perform well in identifying Persian proverbs in context, achieving accuracies above 0.90. However, their performance drops considerably when tasked with identifying equivalent English proverbs, with the best model achieving 0.79 accuracy. Our findings highlight the limitations of current LLMs in cultural knowledge and analogical reasoning, and they provide a framework for assessing cross-cultural understanding in other low-resource languages. MasalBench is available at https://github.com/kalhorghazal/MasalBench.

URLs: https://github.com/kalhorghazal/MasalBench.

replace Language Steering for Multilingual In-Context Learning

Authors: Neeraja Kirtane, Kuan-Hao Huang

Abstract: If multilingual language model representations contain separable language-associated structure, then shifting between languages may require only simple activation-space interventions. To test this hypothesis, we study multilingual in-context learning (ICL) as a controlled setting: few-shot demonstrations are provided in English while the test query is in a target language, creating a clear cross-lingual transfer challenge. We propose language vectors, which are computed as mean activation differences between parallel source and target language examples at a particular layer and applied as inference-time offsets to the model's hidden states. Across three tasks spanning 19 languages and three models, our method improves over English few-shot prompting without any parameter updates. Beyond performance, the vectors encode interpretable linguistic structure: closely related languages form tight clusters, and vectors transfer effectively across tasks and languages, with closely related languages' vectors recovering more of the gain than distant ones. Together, these findings suggest that language occupies structured directions in multilingual activation space, and that inference-time interventions on English demonstrations can narrow the cross-lingual ICL gap.

replace Geometric Alignment without Functional Equivalence: A Layer-wise Analysis of the Speech-Text Modality Gap

Authors: Ming-Hao Hsu, Xiaohai Tian, Jun Zhang, Lu Lu, Yuxuan Wang, Zhizheng Wu

Abstract: End-to-end speech large language models (LLMs) often answer the same question less accurately from speech than from text, even when the spoken content matches the text prompt. Existing analyses study this modality gap through the geometry of representations and measure how closely speech representations align with text. Geometric alignment gives the gap a measurable form, but it does not show whether the model uses aligned representations in the same way. This work connects representation geometry with answer formation and asks whether geometric alignment implies functional equivalence. We compare cross-layer Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) with layer-wise answer margins for four open-weight speech LLMs on SpeechMMLU and VoiceBench BBH. In Qwen2.5-Omni and MiniCPM-o, speech and text representations align in middle layers while their late-layer answer margins still differ, so mid-layer alignment does not imply functional equivalence. Qwen2-Audio instead shows weak alignment, which indicates a different failure mode. An attention intervention at fixed input and token count identifies a causal effect of late attention allocation on answer-code separation, a quantity distinct from answer correctness. Compressing the speech signal or its token sequence repairs a subset of the errors that occur only with speech.

replace LoopRPT: Reinforcement Pre-Training for Looped Language Models

Authors: Guo Tang, Shixin Jiang, Heng Chang, Zihan Zhang, Nuo Chen, Yuhan Li, Huiming Fan, Jia Li, Ming Liu, Bing Qin

Abstract: Looped language models (LoopLMs) perform iterative latent computation to refine internal representations, offering an alternative to explicit chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning. However, existing reinforcement learning (RL) paradigms primarily target output tokens, creating a structural mismatch with looped architectures whose reasoning unfolds implicitly. In this work, we propose LoopRPT, a reinforcement pre-training framework tailored for LoopLMs. By reframing next-token prediction as a next-token reasoning task, LoopRPT assigns reinforcement signals directly to latent steps using an EMA teacher reference and noisy latent rollouts. This formulation enables RL to directly shape intermediate representations, compressing effective reasoning into fewer iterations. We instantiate LoopRPT on Ouro across multiple model scales. Results show that LoopRPT improves per-step representation quality and achieves Pareto dominance in accuracy-computation trade-offs. Large gains on hard tokens indicate that LoopRPT enhances early-stage reasoning rather than merely encouraging premature exits. Our findings highlight reinforcement pre-training as a principled paradigm for learning efficient latent reasoning in LoopLMs.

replace PLACID: Privacy-preserving Large language models for Acronym Clinical Inference and Disambiguation

Authors: Manjushree B. Aithal, Ph. D., Alexander Kotz, James Mitchell, Ph. D

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) offer transformative solutions across many domains, but healthcare integration is hindered by strict data privacy constraints. Clinical narratives are dense with ambiguous acronyms, misinterpretation these abbreviations can precipitate severe outcomes like life-threatening medication errors. While cloud-dependent LLMs excel at Acronym Disambiguation, transmitting Protected Health Information to external servers violates privacy frameworks. To bridge this gap, this study pioneers the evaluation of small-parameter models deployed entirely on-device to ensure privacy preservation. We introduce a privacy-preserving cascaded pipeline leveraging general-purpose local models to detect clinical acronyms, routing them to domain-specific biomedical models for context-relevant expansions. Results reveal that while general instruction-following models achieve high detection accuracy (~0.988), their expansion capabilities plummet (~0.655). Our cascaded approach utilizes domain-specific medical models to increase expansion accuracy to (~0.81). This novel work demonstrates that privacy-preserving, on-device (2B-10B) models deliver high-fidelity clinical acronym disambiguation support.

replace AdaptFuse: Training-Free Sequential Preference Inference via Externalized Bayesian Computation

Authors: Fangzhou Lin, Zilin Dai, Shuo Xing, Peiran Li, Siyuan Yang, Qianwen Ge, Kazunori Yamada, Yang Zhou, Ziming Zhang, Haichong Zhang, Zhengzhong Tu

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) struggle to accumulate evidence across multiple rounds of user interactions, failing to update their beliefs in a manner consistent with Bayesian inference. Existing solutions require fine-tuning on sensitive user interaction data, limiting their applicability in privacy-conscious settings. We propose \textsc{AdaptFuse}, a training-free framework that externalizes probabilistic computation entirely from the LLM, where a symbolic module maintains a Bayesian posterior over a discrete hypothesis set, while a frozen LLM contributes semantic reasoning via multi-sample Dirichlet aggregation. The two signals are combined through entropy-adaptive fusion, which automatically weights each source by its predictive confidence, shifting reliance from the LLM to the symbolic posterior as evidence accumulates. We evaluate across three domains: flight recommendation, hotel recommendation, and web shopping; on Gemma~2 9B, Llama~3 8B, and Qwen~2.5 7B. \textsc{AdaptFuse} consistently outperforms both prompting baselines and fine-tuned Bayesian Teaching models on all tasks, with accuracy improving monotonically over interaction rounds. These results demonstrate that principled inference-time algorithms can substitute for fine-tuning in personalized recommendation, without storing or training on sensitive user data.

replace Phantom Paradoxes: Language Models Treat Unresolved Circular Truth Dependence as Contradiction

Authors: Ji Ho Bae

Abstract: Do language models struggle with self-reference itself, or with structures where propagating truth values along references cannot settle a value in finitely many steps? Our results support the latter. In our main test, statements refer to one another's truth in a cycle, yet exactly one consistent assignment exists. Local propagation never settles the queried value, but exhaustive search finds it, so INCONSISTENT (no consistent assignment) is always wrong. We call such misreadings of satisfiable problems phantom paradoxes. Each circular problem has a grounded twin with the same answer but no cycle. To rule out logical difficulty and unfamiliar wording, we added constraint twins (equivalent formulas without talk of truth) and perplexity-matched grounded controls. In confirmatory behavioral tests fixed before any confirmation response, four open models from three families answered INCONSISTENT on average 31.0 percentage points more often on circular problems than on grounded twins, 36.4 more than on constraint twins and 37.1 more than on perplexity-matched controls. In each model, validated layer-wise truth directions, fitted on unrelated arithmetic, recovered the queried value at the question's last token for grounded twins but not for circular problems. Constraint twins, likewise unrecovered, drew INCONSISTENT far less often, so circular truth dependence, not the unrecovered value alone, matters. In a small natural-language bank, self-reference settling in finitely many steps drew INCONSISTENT 23.4 points less often than unsettled self-reference with the same classical solution. Pooled tests did not show the predicted late-layer revision or attention spread. Overall, models treat unresolved circular truth dependence as contradiction. We will release all prompts, outputs and hidden states.

replace Latent Preference Modeling for Multi-Session Personalized Tool Calling

Authors: Yejin Yoon, Minseo Kim, Taeuk Kim

Abstract: Users often omit essential details in their requests to LLM-based agents, resulting in under-specified inputs for tool use. This poses a fundamental challenge for tool-augmented agents, as API execution typically requires complete arguments, highlighting the need for personalized tool calling. To study this problem, we present Multi-Session Personalized Tool Calling (MPT), a benchmark comprising 4,695 instances over 459 multi-session interaction histories that cover three challenges: Preference Recall, Induction, and Transfer. We further propose PRefine, a test-time memory method that maintains the user's latent preference as a textual hypothesis revised through a generate--verify--refine loop. Across five LLMs, existing memory systems often fail to improve on full-history prompting; PRefine outperforms all baselines and alone surpasses it on Preference Transfer. These results indicate that memory for personalized agents must abstract behavior into preferences, rather than simply archive it.

replace ContextWeaver: Selective and Dependency-Structured Memory Construction for LLM Agents

Authors: Yating Wu, Yuhao Zhang, Sayan Ghosh, Sourya Basu, Anoop Deoras, Jun Huan, Gaurav Gupta

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents often struggle in long-context interactions. As the history grows, sliding windows and prompt compression may drop earlier information that later steps rely on. Retrieval-based memory systems surface relevant content but still overlook the causal and logical dependencies between steps. We introduce ContextWeaver, a selective and dependency-structured memory framework that organizes an agent's interaction trace into a graph of reasoning steps and selects the relevant context for future actions. ContextWeaver supports (1) dependency-based construction and traversal that link each step to the earlier steps it relies on; (2) compact dependency summaries of the reasoning paths; and (3) a lightweight validation layer that incorporates execution feedback. On five software-engineering and terminal benchmarks with a fixed budget per task, ContextWeaver improves the resolve rate over a sliding-window baseline on all five, with a significant gain when pooled across benchmarks, while using 13-18% fewer agent steps. It also performs favorably against alternative context-management approaches, with the largest gains on long-horizon repository tasks. On SWE-Bench Pro, ContextWeaver raises the resolve rate from 66.5% to 72.6%, with significant improvements across all three model backbones. The improvement also holds when the sliding window's budget is raised to cover the summary cost.

replace On Challenges in Evaluation Methodology: A Review

Authors: Ruchira Dhar, Qiwei Peng, Anders S{\o}gaard

Abstract: Recent advances in language models have prompted a growing body of work that questions prevailing evaluation practices. However, many such critiques have already been discussed in natural language processing (NLP), a field with a long history of methodological reflection on evaluation. We conduct a scoping review spanning four decades and develop a taxonomy of 22 recurring concerns. For each concern, we examine how recently it was discussed in the reviewed literature and whether it receives explicit attention in institutional checklists, finding that some concerns have become less visible in literature and many lack coverage in these checklists. Our work provides an overview of the major concerns in evaluation methodology that have shaped the field and identifies potential targets for renewed investigation and field-level intervention by the research community.

replace When Can Digital Personas Reliably Approximate Human Survey Findings?

Authors: Mumin Jia, Yilin Chen, Divya Sharma, Jairo Diaz-Rodriguez

Abstract: Digital personas powered by Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly proposed as substitutes for human survey respondents, yet it remains unclear when they can reliably approximate human survey findings. We answer this question using the LISS panel, constructing personas from respondents' background variables and pre-2023 survey histories, then testing them against the same respondents' held-out post-cutoff answers. Across four persona architectures, three LLMs, and two prediction tasks, we assess performance at the question, respondent, distributional, equity, and clustering levels. Digital personas improve alignment with human response distributions, especially in domains tied to stable attributes and values, but remain limited for individual prediction and fail to recover multivariate respondent structure. Retrieval-augmented architectures provide the clearest gains, but performance depends more on human response structure than on model choice: personas perform best for low-variability questions and common respondent patterns, and worst for subjective, heterogeneous, or rare responses. Our results provide practical guidance on when digital personas could be appropriate for survey research and when human validation remains necessary.

replace When Reasoning Supervision Hurts: TTCW-Based Long-Form Literary Review Generation

Authors: Jinlong Liu, Mohammed Bahja, Mark Lee

Abstract: Automatic evaluation of long-form literary writing remains challenging, particularly for creativity-related dimensions such as originality and flexibility. We address the lack of large-scale supervision for structured Torrance Test of Creative Writing (TTCW)-based evaluation by constructing a dataset of 263{,}911 long-form stories, each paired with scalar scores and meta-synthesised review comments across 14 TTCW-based dimensions. Using this dataset, we study whether reviewer-style reasoning traces improve structured literary review generation across Qwen3-4B, Qwen3-8B, and Nemotron-8B. Across all three models, no-trace supervision yields higher score imitation fidelity and near-complete report generation, whereas trace supervision reduces completion to $85.9$--$90.9\%$. Failure analysis shows that $88.6\%$ of failed reasoning generations contain semantic repetition loops inside the trace. These loops concentrate in particular story--criterion interactions and progressively harden after onset, while comparable repetition is rare in the supervision data. Our results suggest that, for long-form tasks requiring strictly structured outputs, explicit reasoning traces can introduce generation instability that outweighs their overall benefit.

replace ConRAG: Consensus-Driven Evidence Flow for Multi-Hop Question Answering

Authors: Yikai Zhu, Kunfeng Chen, Qihuang Zhong, Juhua Liu, Bo Du

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has emerged as a promising paradigm for enhancing large language models (LLMs) on multi-hop question answering (QA), which requires reasoning over evidence from multiple documents. However, current multi-hop RAG approaches still struggle with evidence selection and information transfer, i.e., retrieving irrelevant passages or failing to pass intermediate information to subsequent reasoning steps, thereby hindering coherent multi-hop reasoning. To this end, we propose ConRAG, a consensus-driven evidence flow framework that effectively boosts LLM performance on complex multi-hop QA. In short, the core of ConRAG is to select relevant evidence via consensus-driven multi-view ranking, and leverage a slot-based mechanism to transfer intermediate information between dependent reasoning steps. Extensive experiments across three multi-hop QA benchmarks show that ConRAG surpasses the baselines by a clear margin. Encouragingly, on Gemma-4-31B-it, ConRAG outperforms the strongest baseline by 5.8 points in average score, while reducing total end-to-end runtime and token consumption by 75.9% and 67.3%, respectively.

replace Robust Asynchronous Planning via Auto-Formalization

Authors: Jiayi Zhang, Jianing Yin, Ben Zhou, Li Zhang

Abstract: LLMs can plan by generating schedules directly as a Planner, or by translating a task into a formal specification for a solver as a Formalizer. Most real-world tasks are asynchronous, with non-uniform durations, concurrency, and execution-time changes, yet existing benchmarks hardly cover them. We unify asynchronous planning under one formulation and release three benchmarks spanning task-graph scale, grounded state and resource constraints, and execution-time events. Surprisingly, PDDL2.1, the formalism built for temporal planning, is the weakest pipeline. On AsyncPlan-XXL it averages 30.8% plan accuracy, against 81.7% for the Planner and 94.0% for a CP-SAT Formalizer. The gap widens with scale, and at 100 actions the three pipelines reach 9.0%, 44.5%, and 82.5%, respectively. The formalism is not to blame. Compiled from the same model-extracted intermediate representation, PDDL2.1 and CP-SAT reach nearly identical accuracy (81.6% vs. 81.5%), and gold task graphs reach 100% for both. What remains is a bottleneck shared by every pipeline: recovering the task graph from language. Execution-time events further degrade every pipeline, yet updating only event-induced constraints reaches 81.6% versus 41.6% for full regeneration. Robust asynchronous planning thus hinges not on the expressiveness of the target formalism but on how reliably a model can write and edit it, and a well-chosen interface can let a weaker model match a stronger one.

replace Ouvia: A User-centered Framework for Measuring Usability of Speech Translation in Real-World Communication Scenarios

Authors: Giuseppe Attanasio, Beatrice Savoldi, Daniel Chechelnitsky, Matteo Negri, Marine Carpuat, Maarten Sap, Andr\'e F. T. Martins

Abstract: Speech translation (ST) is increasingly adopted in user applications, yet its evaluation largely focuses on decontextualized testbeds and holistic quality, rather than end users' communication needs. We introduce Ouvia, an evaluation framework for measuring user-perceived usability of speech translation outputs in real-world settings. Ouvia focuses on one-to-one communication: an English speaker needs to convey a request to a Portuguese speaker, and the message is automatically translated. Through a custom web app and multi-phase study design, we collect more than 1,750 such interactions in healthcare and everyday situations, mediated by four ST systems, involving speakers from three English dialects and two genders. We find that modern ST serves people only to a limited extent---only around half of interactions are rated as usable---with significant gaps in reported usability across demographic groups. Moreover, among quality metrics, we find that QA-based evaluation is a substantially stronger predictor of real-world usability than standard approaches. Together, these findings stress the importance of situated, user-centered evaluation frameworks that go beyond holistic quality scores and attend to who the technology serves---and how well.

replace Explain Like I'm 5 or Whatever I Choose: Evaluating the Potential for Interactive Complexity in Language Model Responses

Authors: Indu Panigrahi, Tal August

Abstract: Given the increased use of large language models (LLMs) for reading and seeking information and the varying needs and backgrounds of users, direct manipulation of text can be a valuable mechanism for end-user control. However, enabling direct manipulation requires that models generate text consistent with user-requested changes. We focus on one such setting, manipulating the complexity of scientific information, and ask if models can generate multiple summaries along a continuum of text complexity that enable effective user selection and control. Through a within-subjects user study, we identify reliable complexity measures and a criterion for how models should conform to these measures.
