replace-cross The Invisible Leash: Why RLVR May or May Not Escape Its Origin

Authors: Fang Wu, Weihao Xuan, Danny Xing, Ximing Lu, Mingjie Liu, Yi Dong, Zaid Harchaoui, Yejin Choi

Abstract: Recent advances highlight Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) as a promising approach to enhancing LLM capabilities. However, it remains unclear whether the current practice of RLVR truly expands models' reasoning boundary or mainly amplifies high-reward outputs that base models already know. We introduce empirical support dynamics, a framework that quantifies how RLVR preserves, expands, and shrinks access to verified solutions. Applied across models, reasoning tasks, and training checkpoints, it matches verified successes question by question to reveal changes that aggregate accuracy can conceal. Across the evaluated model-level benchmark aggregates, \textit{the shrinkage of observed support outweighs its expansion}: more questions lose a verified success than gain one at the stated sampling budgets. These losses occur alongside gains in \texttt{pass@1}. Some RLVR models increase average token-level entropy while reducing final-answer entropy: their token choices become more uncertain on average even as their final answers become more concentrated. Together, these findings reveal an ``invisible leash'' in current RLVR practice: it can make existing successes more reliable while losing access to other verified solutions. Empirical support dynamics make this imbalance measurable and motivate exploration strategies that reach underrepresented solutions while retaining prior successes.

replace-cross Ground-Truth Subgraphs for Better Training and Evaluation of Knowledge Graph Augmented LLMs

Authors: Alberto Cattaneo, Carlo Luschi, Daniel Justus

Abstract: Retrieval of information from graph-structured knowledge bases represents a promising direction for improving the factuality of LLMs. While various solutions have been proposed, a comparison of methods is difficult due to the lack of challenging QA datasets with ground-truth targets for graph retrieval. We present SynthKGQA, an LLM-powered framework for generating high-quality Knowledge Graph Question Answering datasets from any Knowledge Graph, providing the full set of ground-truth facts in the KG to reason over questions. To demonstrate its utility, we apply SynthKGQA to ogbl-wikikg2, a Wikidata-derived KG, and validate generated items against an up-to-date dump of Wikidata to create GTSQA. This new dataset is specifically designed to test zero-shot generalization with respect to unseen graph structures and relation types, enabling us to analyze the abilities and limitations of SOTA graph retrieval approaches at an unprecedented level of granularity. We also show that KG retrievers trained on GTSQA can transfer to a human-curated benchmark, and that the ground-truth subgraphs produced by SynthKGQA provide a better training supervision signal than previously-used heuristics.

replace-cross The Instability of Safety: How Random Seeds and Temperature Expose Inconsistent LLM Refusal Behavior

Authors: Erik Larsen

Abstract: Current safety evaluations of large language models rely on single-shot testing, implicitly assuming that model responses are deterministic and representative of the model's safety alignment. We challenge this assumption by investigating the stability of safety refusal decisions across random seeds and temperature settings. Testing four instruction-tuned models from three families (Llama 3.1 8B, Qwen 2.5 7B, Qwen 3 8B, Gemma 3 12B) on 876 harmful prompts across 20 sampling configurations (4 temperatures x 5 seeds), we find that 18-28% of prompts exhibit decision flips (the REFUSE/PARTIAL/COMPLY label changes across configurations), depending on the model. Our Safety Stability Index (SSI) reveals that higher temperatures significantly reduce decision stability (Friedman chi-squared = 396.81, p < 0.001), with mean within-temperature SSI dropping from 0.977 at temperature 0.0 to 0.942 at temperature 1.0. We validate findings across all model families using Claude 3.5 Haiku as a unified external judge, achieving 89.1% inter-judge agreement with the Llama 70B judge on the two models both judges labeled (Cohen's kappa = 0.62). Within each model, prompts with higher compliance rates exhibit lower stability (Spearman rho = -0.47 to -0.70, all p < 0.001), indicating that models "waver" more on borderline requests. These findings demonstrate that single-shot safety evaluations are insufficient for reliable safety assessment and that evaluation protocols must account for stochastic variation in model behavior. For Llama 3.1 8B, single-shot evaluation agrees with multi-sample ground truth only 92.5% of the time when pooling across temperatures (98.7% at greedy to 90.3% at temperature 1.0), and we recommend scaling samples with temperature--one at greedy, three at low temperature, more at higher temperatures (where three reach only ~95%), and ten when pooling--rather than a single flat threshold.

replace-cross Dual-Evolving Reinforcement Learning for Automated Reward Design

Authors: Sitao Cheng, Tianle Li, Xuhan Huang, Xunjian Yin, Difan Zou

Abstract: Crafting effective reward signals remains a central challenge in reinforcement learning (RL) for complex reasoning. Existing methods for automated reward optimization search with a fixed proposer which never learns from its costly trials. We present Dual-Evolving Reinforcement Learning (DERL), a dual-training framework for autonomous discovery of reward structures. An outer-loop proposer composes a reward from task primitives, and an inner policy trains with this reward. The proposer itself learns by policy gradient from the validation performance of the inner policy. Thus, DERL utilizes past-trial feedback to shape its reward-proposal distribution, an empirical meta-gradient of task success. We validate DERL on embodied (ALFWorld), scientific (ScienceWorld), and mathematical (GSM8K, MATH) reasoning. On multi-turn agent benchmarks, DERL outperforms strong baselines, significantly on out-of-distribution tasks. In mathematics, DERL improves on the outcome reward where directly added primitives hurt. Further analyses under matched compute show that our proposer effectively captures the meta-gradient to discover useful reward signals.

replace-cross SWE-Spot: Building Small Repo-Experts with Repository-Centric Learning

Authors: Jinjun Peng, Magnus Saebo, Tianjun Zhong, Junfeng Yang, Shi-Xiong Zhang, Baishakhi Ray, Simin Chen, Yangruibo Ding

Abstract: The deployment of coding agents in privacy-sensitive and resource-constrained environments drives the demand for capable open-weight Small Language Models (SLMs). However, they suffer from a fundamental capability gap: unlike frontier large models, they lack the inference-time strong generalization to handle complicated, unfamiliar codebases. We identify that the prevailing Task-Centric Learning (TCL) approach, which scales exposure across disparate repositories, fails to address this limitation. In response, we propose Repository-Centric Learning (RCL), a shift that prioritizes vertical, single-repository depth over horizontal, single-task breadth for SLM training. To accomplish this depth, we design a four-unit Repository-Centric Experience, transforming static codebases into interactive learning signals, to train SWE-Spot-4B, a family of highly compact models built as repo-specialized experts. Under controlled comparisons with the same teacher and data budget, RCL consistently outperforms TCL across tasks, teachers, model scales, and programming languages. On seven target repositories, SWE-Spot-4B experts match or exceed existing open-weight models up to $8\times$ larger (e.g., CWM by Meta, Qwen3-Coder-30B) and surpass or match efficiency-focused commercial models (e.g., GPT-4.1-mini, GPT-5-nano) across multiple SWE tasks. Further analysis reveals that RCL yields higher training and inference efficiency, deep knowledge acquisition, and can scale to repository-cluster experts. Our work emphasizes that for building efficient intelligence, repository mastery is a distinct, necessary, and practical dimension that complements general coding capability.

replace-cross When Is Enough Not Enough? Illusory Completion in Search Agents

Authors: Dayoon Ko, Jihyuk Kim, Sohyeon Kim, Haeju Park, Dahyun Lee, Gunhee Kim, Moontae Lee, Kyungjae Lee

Abstract: In agentic search, an LLM agent searches the web, reads the pages it finds, and decides what to look for next before returning an answer. But can we trust an answer simply because the agent returns it? Often not, and even a correct answer can be a lucky guess: on questions with several constraints, we find that agents conclude the task is complete while a constraint remains unverified in up to 48% of their correct answers. We call this illusory completion. To see how it arises, we introduce the Epistemic Ledger, which tracks at every turn what the retrieved pages establish about each constraint and what the agent claims. Across 13 agents, from 7B RL-trained models to frontier LLMs, training and scale raise accuracy but change the pattern of verification failures rather than eliminating them: constraints may be left unchecked, assumed without support, or retained despite refuting evidence. To measure what agents lose without tracking their constraints, we show them each constraint's state, approximated by LiveLedger, a lightweight 4B tracker. Agents then answer 4.4-16.1 points more questions correctly, suggesting that on their own, they may not track what they have verified and what remains.

replace-cross Semantic Communities and Boundary-Spanning Lyrics in K-pop: A Graph-Based Unsupervised Analysis

Authors: Oktay Karaku\c{s}, Can Ey\"upo\u{g}lu

Abstract: We examine how lyric representation shapes computational similarity using four 384-dimensional multilingual MiniLM representations of 7,891 K-pop songs by 1,456 artists from charts spanning 2000 to October 2023. Whole-song truncated embeddings provide the strongest standardized separation between neighbouring and random song pairs (1.995), compared with 1.931 for chunked documents, although chunked representations show greater adjusted Rand agreement and line-based representations yield higher modularity. Using whole-song embeddings, we construct a weighted cosine nearest-neighbour graph ($k=30$). A representative Louvain partition identifies 21 operational communities, without a corresponding robust plateau in the Markov Stability analysis. AI-assisted examination of selected lyrics and terms suggests overlapping patterns of memory, separation, longing, self-reflection, attraction, and performance. Community membership is associated with character-script composition (Cram\'er's $V=0.294$; permutation $p=0.001$). High-participation songs exhibit broader cross-community connectivity, while participation has weak negative associations with embedding-relative novelty. These findings show how representation choices and textual characteristics influence the network organization of a cultural corpus, subject to limitations from truncation, graph construction, and the absence of independent human semantic validation.

replace-cross Draft-Thinking: Learning Efficient Reasoning in Long Chain-of-Thought LLMs

Authors: Jie Cao, Tianwei Lin, Juekai Lin, Zhenxuan Fan, Yunqi Cao, Bo Yuan, Ziyuan Zhao, Rolan Yan, Wenqiao Zhang, Siliang Tang

Abstract: Long chain-of-thought~(CoT) reasoning improves the problem-solving capability of large reasoning models~(LRMs), but often incurs substantial inference cost and overthinking. Existing efficiency methods primarily rely on prompting, post-hoc compression, or length-regularized optimization; these approaches can be sensitive to model scale, depend on external estimators, or under-allocate reasoning to difficult instances. We propose \textbf{Draft-Thinking}, a lightweight adaptation framework that learns a \textit{model-adapted draft CoT}: a compact, self-contained working derivation that directly supports the final answer. We identify a \textbf{Cross-Scale Draft Mismatch}: concise traces generated by a strong teacher can be too sparse for a smaller target model. To address this mismatch, \textbf{Progressive Draft Adaptation} first transfers a concise reasoning-form prior through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and then applies two-stage reinforcement learning (RL) with progressively harder data and relaxed response budgets to calibrate the reasoning granularity to the target model. The method requires neither post-hoc trajectory compression nor an explicit length-penalty reward. At inference time, draft, adaptive, and step-by-step prompts provide efficiency-oriented, balanced, and detailed reasoning modes, respectively. The step-by-step mode largely preserves the original model's accuracy while using fewer tokens. With only 847 training problems and 120 RL optimization steps, Draft-Thinking substantially reduces reasoning cost across six mathematical and scientific reasoning benchmarks. On MATH500, its draft mode reduces the average response length from 5,668 to 986 tokens, an 82.6\% reduction, while achieving 90.6\% accuracy compared with 93.0\% for the original long CoT model.

replace-cross TimeWarp: Evaluating Web Agents by Revisiting the Past

Authors: Md Farhan Ishmam, Kenneth Marino

Abstract: As web agents close the gap with humans on benchmarks, one question arises: Do today's agents perform just as well on tomorrow's web? We introduce TimeWarp, a benchmark that emulates the evolving web. TimeWarp consists of three web environments, each with six UI versions spanning UI design, frontend code, and workflows from different eras of the internet. We pair TimeWarp with a set of complex, realistic tasks covering different forms of web navigation. Our experiments reveal that vision-based agents are vulnerable to changes, while text-based agents become brittle once fine-tuned on a single version. To address this, we propose TimeTraj, a new annotation method that uses plan distillation to collect trajectories across multiple versions. By training agents on teacher rollouts using our BC-variant, we achieve substantial performance gains: 20.4% to 37.7% for Qwen-3 4B and 0% to 27.0% for Llama-3.1 8B models. Our work helps study generalization across web designs and opens a new paradigm for collecting plans rather than trajectories to improve the robustness of web agents.

replace-cross The Reasoning Trap -- Logical Reasoning as a Mechanistic Pathway to Situational Awareness

Authors: Subramanyam Sahoo, Aman Chadha, Vinija Jain, Divya Chaudhary

Abstract: Situational awareness, the capacity of an AI system to recognize its own nature, understand its training and deployment context, and reason strategically about its circumstances, is widely considered among the most dangerous emergent capabilities in advanced AI systems. Separately, a growing research effort seeks to improve the logical reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) across deduction, induction, and abduction. In this paper, we argue that these two research trajectories are on a collision course. We introduce the RAISE framework (Reasoning Advancing Into Self Examination), which identifies three mechanistic pathways through which improvements in logical reasoning enable progressively deeper levels of situational awareness: deductive self inference, inductive context recognition, and abductive self modeling. We formalize each pathway, construct an escalation ladder from basic self recognition to strategic deception, and demonstrate that every major research topic in LLM logical reasoning maps directly onto a specific amplifier of situational awareness. We further analyze why current safety measures are insufficient to prevent this escalation. We conclude by proposing concrete safeguards, including a "Mirror Test" benchmark and a Reasoning Safety Parity Principle, and pose an uncomfortable but necessary question to the logical reasoning community about its responsibility in this trajectory.

replace-cross BenchPreS: A Benchmark for Context-Aware Personalized Preference Selectivity of Persistent-Memory LLMs

Authors: Sangyeon Yoon, Hyesoo Hong, Wonje Jeung, Yongil Kim, Wooseok Seo, Heuiyeen Yeen, Albert No, Sunkyoung Kim

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly store user preferences in persistent memory to support personalization across interactions. However, in third-party communication settings governed by social and institutional norms, some user preferences may be inappropriate to apply. We introduce BenchPreS, which evaluates whether memory-based user preferences are appropriately applied or suppressed across communication contexts. Using two complementary metrics, Misapplication Rate (MR) and Appropriate Application Rate (AAR), we find even frontier LLMs struggle to apply preferences in a context-sensitive manner. Models with stronger preference adherence exhibit higher rates of over-application, and neither reasoning capability nor prompt-based defenses fully resolve this issue. These results suggest current LLMs treat personalized preferences as globally enforceable rules rather than as context-dependent normative signals.

replace-cross Terminal Agents Suffice for Enterprise Automation

Authors: Patrice Bechard, Orlando Marquez Ayala, Emily Chen, Jordan Skelton, Sagar Davasam, Srinivas Sunkara, Vikas Yadav, Sai Rajeswar

Abstract: There has been growing interest in building agents that can interact with digital platforms to execute meaningful enterprise tasks autonomously. Among the approaches explored are tool-augmented agents built on abstractions such as Model Context Protocol (MCP) and web agents that operate through graphical interfaces. Yet, it remains unclear whether such complex agentic systems are necessary given their cost and operational overhead. We argue that a coding agent equipped only with a terminal and a filesystem can solve many enterprise tasks more effectively by interacting directly with platform APIs. We evaluate this hypothesis across diverse real-world systems and show that these low-level terminal agents match or outperform more complex agent architectures at a fraction of the cost. Our findings suggest that simple, flexible programmatic interfaces combined with strong foundation models should be the backbone of enterprise automation.

replace-cross Reward Design for Physical Reasoning in Vision-Language Models

Authors: Derek Lilienthal, Manisha Mukherjee, Sameera Horawalavithana

Abstract: Physical reasoning over images requires integrating perception, domain knowledge, and multi-step symbolic inference, yet Vision-Language Models (VLMs) fall far short of human performance on physics benchmarks. Reinforcement learning can improve reasoning via reward signals, but it remains unclear whether these signals must come from outside the model or can be derived from the model itself. We compare external rewards, which rely on an outside verifier (rule-based format and answer-correctness checks, or an LLM-judge rubric), with an internal attention-based reward computed from the model's own attention over image regions, requiring no labels or judge. We find that external rewards drive most of the accuracy gains, while preserving more coherent reasoning when rewarded for reasoning tokens. The internal attention-based reward, provides an annotation-free grounding signal that improves spatial-relation reasoning and is complementary to external rewards: adding it to format and accuracy rewards, at no additional annotation cost, improves average accuracy on Molmo~2 while generalizing to out-of-distribution tasks. Our results suggest that internal attention rewards can supplement external supervision and lead to improved physical reasoning.

replace-cross Omni-Embed-Audio: Leveraging Multimodal LLMs for Robust Audio-Text Retrieval

Authors: HaeJun Yoo, Yongseop Shin, Insung Lee, Myoung-Wan Koo, Du-Seong Chang

Abstract: Audio-text retrieval systems based on Contrastive Language-Audio Pretraining (CLAP) achieve strong performance on traditional benchmarks; however, these benchmarks rely on caption-style queries that differ substantially from real-world search behavior, limiting their assessment of practical retrieval robustness. We present Omni-Embed-Audio (OEA), a retrieval-oriented encoder leveraging multimodal LLMs with native audio understanding. To systematically evaluate robustness beyond caption-style queries, we introduce User-Intent Queries (UIQs) - five formulations reflecting natural search behaviors: questions, commands, keyword tags, paraphrases, and exclusion-based negative queries. For negative queries, we develop a hard negative mining pipeline and propose discrimination metrics (HNSR, TFR) assessing models' ability to suppress acoustically similar distractors. Experiments on AudioCaps, Clotho, and MECAT show that OEA achieves comparable text-to-audio retrieval performance to state-of-the-art M2D-CLAP, while demonstrating clear advantages in two critical areas: (1) dominant text-to-text retrieval (+22% relative improvement), and (2) substantially superior hard negative discrimination (+4.3%p HNSR@10, +34.7% relative TFR@10), revealing that LLM backbones provide superior semantic understanding of complex queries.

replace-cross Document-as-Image Representations Fall Short for Scientific Retrieval

Authors: Ghazal Khalighinejad, Raghuveer Thirukovalluru, Alexander H. Oh, Bhuwan Dhingra

Abstract: Many recent document embedding models are trained on document-as-image representations, embedding rendered pages as images rather than the underlying source. Meanwhile, existing benchmarks for scientific document retrieval, such as ArXivQA and ViDoRe, treat documents as images of pages, implicitly favoring such representations. In this work, we argue that this paradigm is not well-suited for text-rich multimodal scientific documents, where critical evidence is distributed across structured sources, including text, tables, and figures. To study this setting, we introduce ArXivDoc, a new benchmark constructed from the underlying LaTeX sources of scientific papers. Unlike PDF or image-based representations, LaTeX provides direct access to structured elements (e.g., sections, tables, figures, equations), enabling controlled query construction grounded in specific evidence types. We systematically compare text-only, image-based, and multimodal representations across both single-vector and multi-vector retrieval models. Our results show that: (1) document-as-image representations are consistently suboptimal, especially as document length increases; (2) text-based representations are most effective, even for figure-based queries, by leveraging captions and surrounding context; and (3) interleaved text+image representations outperform document-as-image approaches without requiring specialized training.

replace-cross Ideological Bias in LLMs' Economic Causal Reasoning

Authors: Donggyu Lee, Hyeok Yun, Jungwon Kim, Junsik Min, Sungwon Park, Sangyoon Park, Jihee Kim

Abstract: Do large language models (LLMs) exhibit systematic ideological bias when reasoning about economic causal effects? As LLMs are increasingly used in policy analysis and economic reporting, where directionally correct causal judgments are essential, this question has direct practical stakes. We present a systematic evaluation by extending the EconCausal benchmark with ideology-contested cases - instances where intervention-oriented (pro-government) and market-oriented (pro-market) perspectives predict divergent causal signs. From 10,490 causal triplets (treatment-outcome pairs with empirically verified effect directions) derived from top-tier economics and finance journals, we identify 1,056 ideology-contested instances and evaluate 20 state-of-the-art LLMs on their ability to predict empirically supported causal directions. We find that ideology-contested items are consistently harder than non-contested ones, and that across 17 of 20 models, accuracy is systematically higher when the empirically verified causal sign aligns with intervention-oriented expectations than with market-oriented ones. Moreover, when models err, their incorrect predictions disproportionately lean intervention-oriented, and this directional skew is not eliminated by one-shot in-context prompting. These results highlight that LLMs are not only less accurate on ideologically contested economic questions, but systematically less reliable in one ideological direction than the other, underscoring the need for direction-aware evaluation in high-stakes economic and policy settings.

replace-cross OScaR: The Occam's Razor for Extreme KV Cache Quantization in LLMs and Beyond

Authors: Zunhai Su, Rui Yang, Chao Zhang, Yaxiu Liu, Yifan Zhang, Wei Wu, Jing Xiong, Dayou Du, Xialie Zhuang, Yulei Qian, Jianchao Tan, Yuchen Xie, Yik-Chung Wu, Hongxia Yang, Ngai Wong

Abstract: The rapid advancement toward long-context reasoning and multi-modal intelligence has made the memory footprint of the Key-Value (KV) cache a dominant memory bottleneck for efficient deployment. While the established per-channel quantization effectively accommodates intrinsic channel-wise outliers in Key tensors, its efficacy diminishes under extreme compression. In this work, we revisit the inherent limitations of the per-channel quantization paradigm from both empirical and theoretical perspectives. Our analysis identifies Token Norm Imbalance (TNI) as the primary bottleneck to quantization fidelity. We demonstrate that TNI systematically amplifies errors when shared quantization parameters are required to span token groups exhibiting substantial norm disparities. Instead of relying on intricate quantization pipelines (e.g., TurboQuant), we propose OScaR (Omni-Scaled Canalized Rotation), an accurate and lightweight KV cache compression framework for X-LLMs (i.e., text-only, multi-modal, and omni-modal LLMs). Advancing the per-channel paradigm, OScaR employs Canalized Rotation followed by Omni-Token Scaling to mitigate TNI-induced sequence-dimensional variance both effectively and efficiently, further supported by our optimized system design and CUDA kernels. Extensive evaluations across X-LLMs show that OScaR consistently outperforms existing methods and achieves near-lossless performance under INT2 quantization, establishing it as a robust, low-complexity, and universal framework that defines a new Pareto front. Compared with the BF16 FlashDecoding-v2 baseline, our OScaR implementation achieves a notable up to 3.0x speedup in decoding, reduces memory footprint by 5.3x, and increases throughput by 4.1x. The code for OScaR is publicly available at https://github.com/ZunhaiSu/OScaR-KV-Quant.

URLs: https://github.com/ZunhaiSu/OScaR-KV-Quant.

replace-cross T2J-BENCH: Benchmarking Coding Agents on Execution-Grounded PyTorch-to-JAX Repository Migration

Authors: Tao Feng, Jiefeng Chen, Linxin Song, Yufan Zhuang, Yue Huang, Bhavana Dalvi Mishra, Chi Wang, Xiangru Tang, Jieyu Zhao, Ge Liu, Tomas Pfister, Jinsung Yoon

Abstract: Migrating a machine-learning codebase across frameworks--for example from PyTorch to JAX--is a routine but costly engineering task that is fundamentally cross-paradigm: eager, stateful, object-oriented modules must be rewritten as pure, jittable functions over an explicit parameter pytree. We ask whether coding agents can perform such whole-repository migrations correctly, and argue that existing code benchmarks, which grade unit-test pass rates or surface-level similarity to a reference translation, cannot answer this: they neither require the migrated system to \emph{behave} the same nor prevent a JAX reimplementation from faking equivalence by secretly importing PyTorch itself. We introduce T2J-BENCH, a benchmark of 63 whole-repository PyTorch-to-JAX migration tasks spanning 18 model families across both training and serving, where torch-free checkpoint conversion is itself part of the deliverable. T2J-BENCH grades execution-grounded functional equivalence: each candidate is run inside a separate, torch-free, offline verifier and scored against references frozen from the PyTorch original, under a hard independence gate and a continuous, threshold-free reward. Across twenty off-the-shelf agent--model configurations, the strongest recovers only 0.676 of the reward against a 0.980 oracle, with substantial numeric and behavioral divergence despite high spec pass rates. Finally, we contribute TWINHARNESS, a reference-grounded agent harness that turns the agent's executable PyTorch source into legitimate self-verification feedback; applied to an opus-5 agent it raises the reward from 0.649 to 0.723--the highest of any configuration--using only task-visible resources.

replace-cross Learning Reusable Agent Subroutines from Experience

Authors: Zhihan Lei, Jiarui Yan, Joshua Momo, William W. Cohen

Abstract: Existing methods induce various forms of knowledge from agent traces, from sample workflows to programmatic skills. We propose ARISE (Agent Routine Induction from Successful Experience), which identifies recurring procedures in agent traces and synthesizes them into a reusable library of agent subroutines. Across five tasks in narrative deduction, rule application, and constraint planning, it is shown to be a more effective learning strategy than prompting methods and prose and code artifact induction methods. ARISE outperforms chain-of-thought in all ten model-task settings of the main evaluation. Compared to state-of-the-art methods in agent experience induction, our method scores 6.1 percentage points over the strongest evaluated baselines, and 11.5 percentage points over code-skill induction. In our controlled experiment, ARISE also improves average accuracy by up to 15.6 percentage points compared to formatting the induced knowledge as skills and by up to 19.1 points over rewriting it as code tools.

replace-cross AgentWorld: A Data Factory for Training LLM Agents from Multi-Turn Interactions

Authors: Daoyu Wang, Mingyue Cheng, Qingchuan Li, Shuo Yu, Jie Ouyang, Qi Liu, Enhong Chen

Abstract: Training LLMs to act as capable agents requires learning from multi-turn interactions with environments. However, multi-turn interaction trajectories generated by heterogeneous agent runtimes are difficult to transform into structured, high-quality training data that training engines can readily consume. In this paper, we propose AgentWorld, a data factory that bridges heterogeneous agent runtimes and training engines through a Gateway Server and a Data Pool, reframing LLM agent training around a unified data lifecycle from interaction collection to training consumption. The Gateway Server captures multi-turn interactions through an LLM API proxy, while the Data Pool organizes them into standardized step records for curation and training. An interactive dashboard supports trajectory monitoring, step inspection, and data review. In our demonstration, users integrate ready-to-use white-box and black-box agents, trace and curate their interaction data across the pipeline, and extend the factory to new agents and datasets through lightweight configuration. Quantitative experiments across multiple datasets and RL methods demonstrate training support for different agent runtimes. AgentWorld provides a practical foundation for transforming heterogeneous multi-turn experience into training-ready data for LLM agents. Our code is available at https://github.com/AgentR1/Claw-R1 and the demonstration video can be found at link https://youtu.be/Pw47dAOw6B0.

replace-cross Misinformation Propagation in Benign Multi-Agent Systems

Authors: Jonas Becker, Jan Philip Wahle, Terry Ruas, Bela Gipp

Abstract: Multi-agent systems (MAS) are credited with solving complex problems, yet their agents routinely act on context that may be false. Prior work shows single LLMs are swayed by false context, and MAS can be compromised by adversarial agents persuading peers. We study the benign case: agents follow one protocol, none intends to deceive, but some read a misleading passage. We study it with MINT, 10,278 LLM-written misinformation passages in eight styles, testing three open-weight models on commonsense reasoning, knowledge QA, and moral judgment. A relevant false passage costs a single agent 0.12-0.43 accuracy; irrelevant or content-free passages typically cost at most 0.06. Debate looks robust because uninformed agents outnumber misinformed ones. With one of three misinformed, it is no better than choosing the majority over equally exposed independent samples. Interaction helps only with a misinformed majority, recovering up to 0.24 accuracy over voting (0.29 under consensus). A one-sentence unreliability warning recovers up to 0.18 accuracy under voting (0.28 under consensus), at no significant cost under voting when context is true. Robustness should be judged against exposure-matched baselines, with prompt-level skepticism as cheap first defense.

replace-cross From Clicks to Intent: Cross-Platform Session Embeddings with LLM-Distilled Taxonomy for Financial Services Recommendations

Authors: Dianjing Fan, Yao Li, Kyaw Hpone Myint, Dwipam Katariya, Alexandre G. R. Day, Pranab Mohanty, Giri Iyengar

Abstract: Sequential user behavior modeling is widely adopted in industrial recommender systems; however, significant gaps remain in financial services, where pre-login web interactions and authenticated in-app experiences differ drastically. Specifically, pre-login web users typically explore new products, whereas logged-in app users focus on account servicing. Due to the challenge of cross-channel entity resolution (e.g., matching anonymous web sessions to authenticated mobile accounts), web-based intent signals remain underutilized for post-authentication personalization. Existing methods for capturing web-based intent are often ad-hoc and narrow, lacking the flexibility to support both quantitative downstream recommendations and qualitative understanding at scale. In this work, we propose a scalable and dual-purpose intent prediction framework for web-based interactions and demonstrate its applicability for personalization. Our approach transforms raw web clickstreams into two outputs: a self-supervised Transformer encodes multi-modal clickstreams into a compact session embedding, while an LLM-based taxonomy generation and distillation pipeline produces interpretable intent labels. Our system demonstrates that self-supervised clickstream representations combined with LLM-distilled taxonomies can jointly serve quantitative tasks and qualitative understanding in production: on the mobile homepage tile ranking task, the session embedding improves macro Recall@1 by 1.88% and reduces Log Loss by 13.38% over production baselines. On the user conversion prediction task, the embedding outperforms the LLM labels by 4.3% on micro F1, while the distillation layer delivers interpretable labels at ultra-low latency with only a 7% performance drop.

replace-cross Algorithmic Unverifiability of Safety for Fixed and Recursively Self-Improving Systems

Authors: Jose Pascual Gumbau Mezquita

Abstract: Existing impossibility results for AI verification concern a fixed system. Self-modifying systems (agents that rewrite their own code, recursive self-improvement loops) raise a different question: can a safety property that holds now be certified, in advance, to keep holding after the system rewrites itself? We model one step of self-modification as a total computable transformation of code, and preservation as 'safe now and safe after the step'. If the transformation depends only on behaviour, preservation is Rice's theorem one level up; if it reads the code, as self-modification does, preservation is no longer semantic, yet at least as hard as the halting problem, by the same s-m-n reduction carried out inside a class of behaviourally identical programs. One step is never harder than the property; persistence along the whole trajectory can be one arithmetical level higher. No tower of supervisors lowers the problem; every total supervisor errs on a set of systems it cannot locate. Every faithful scheme certifying on finite behavioural evidence admits, for properties not settled by any finite set of inputs, an evolution trace it certifies at every stage while the property is violated. Statically, we record the classical limits (Rice, Goedel, Trakhtenbrot) and add the resource face: over succinctly described finite environments, verifying a policy against an adversary is coNP-complete and synthesising one PSPACE-complete. These limits do not say self-modifying systems are unsafe; they locate where algorithmic guarantees break, at the expressivity a general system needs: Turing-completeness and unrestricted self-modification. What survives is exact: a monitor detects the first violation of a safety property, and no sound procedure certifies all safe systems; certification requires restricting rewrites by design; and a frozen reference keeps the question well posed.

replace-cross What Does a Discrete Diffusion Model Learn? One Reverse Process in Three Coordinates

Authors: Rodrigo Casado Noguerales, Bernhard Sch\"olkopf, Thomas Hofmann, Aran Raoufi

Abstract: What does a discrete diffusion model actually learn? The literature trains denoisers, bridge plug-ins, and concrete scores, each deriving its own ELBO objective under different conventions. This fragmentation makes the object being learned unclear and ELBO values difficult to interpret and compare. All three methods learn the same object in different coordinates: the exact time-reversal of the noising process. We first prove the Oracle Distance identity: for any noising process, the negative ELBO is not just a bound on the likelihood; it equals the data entropy plus the path KL divergence from the reversed noising process to the learned one. Next, for token-factorizing noise, we show that denoiser, bridge plug-in, and concrete score are three coordinates of this reverse process, derive exact conversions between them, and identify the probability distribution learned by each. In particular, contrary to its standard interpretation, the bridge plug-in learns not a denoiser but the cavity distribution: the clean token given the noisy sequence excluding its own position. We confirm the theory by training all three coordinates with masked, uniform, and GIDD kernels on OpenWebText under a shared recipe, and by training uniform-diffusion solvers on four logic tasks. The learned coordinates agree after conversion, while confusing the cavity with the denoiser can severely degrade sampling quality. Together with exact boundary-term formulas, closed-form NELBO calibrations at initialization, and a clock interpretation of time schedules, these results provide a conceptual framework and a mechanical recipe for deriving, implementing, and comparing discrete diffusion processes.

replace-cross CausalDS: Benchmarking Causal Reasoning in Data-Science Agents

Authors: Andrej Leban, Theo Saarinen, Yuekai Sun

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly act as data-science agents, combining abstract reasoning with advanced tool use. Yet the relevant benchmark landscape largely divides into symbolic causal reasoning benchmarks without realistic data analysis or data analysis benchmarks without causal data-generating structure or a range of causal data science tasks. Existing causal evaluation datasets are often restricted to curated examples from existing sources, with diversity coming from limited templatized variations rather than from systematic generation of novel synthetic causal structures. We introduce CausalDS, a benchmark for evaluating causal reasoning in agentic data-science workflows. Each benchmark instance is a scene consisting of a sampled structural causal model (SCM) with generated observational data and an accompanying synthetic natural-language story grounded in a real domain. We ground the composition of the benchmark components in empirical distributions obtained from real-world datasets, thus retaining empirical structure while reducing the memorization risk through completely synthetic generation. From each scene, we then derive tasks spanning all three of Pearl's rungs. Most tasks include a data science coding component, where the model typically needs to use several tools to arrive at the final answer due to imperfect observations. Abstention is tested directly: for targets the graph does not identify, the graded answer is to abstain. The benchmark thus jointly evaluates symbolic causal reasoning, data science, uncertainty quantification, abstention, and tool use/coding. Because the generator is fully synthetic, the exam is not a fixed dataset: composition, difficulty, and size are parameters, and fresh exams can be drawn at will.

replace-cross SPARK: Learning from Verification in Speculative GRPO Rollouts

Authors: Nhat Minh Pham, Duy Tung Doan, Quang Huy Phung, Thi Duyen Ngo, Vinh Van Nguyen, Khac-Hoai Nam Bui

Abstract: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) has become a standard approach for improving the reasoning abilities of large language models, but its training is costly because each step requires generating many long reasoning responses. Recent methods use speculative decoding to accelerate the rollouts, where a draft model proposes tokens that the target model verifies. However, we find that the draft model repeatedly misses the same tokens within a response and across responses in the same GRPO group, and the verification feedback on these misses is not used to update the draft during the rollout. Motivated by this analysis, we introduce SPARK, a lightweight self-adaptive mechanism designed to enhance draft models used in speculative GRPO training. SPARK leverages target verification feedback to refine draft predictions during generation while preserving their existing training procedures. Averaged across 21 model-dataset settings, SPARK improves generation speedup by 32.7% and 11.4%, and end-to-end GRPO training speedup by 32.2% and 8.5%, over EAGLE3-style and DFlash-style baselines, respectively.

replace-cross Rubric Dropout: A Simple Way to Mitigate Reward Hacking in Rubric-as-Reward RL

Authors: Minglai Yang, Xinyu Guo, Zhengliang Shi, Utkarsh Tyagi, Mian Zhang, Razvan Dumitru, Sunjie Hou, Yunzhong He, Daniel Yue Zhang, Ying Liu, Dan Roth

Abstract: Rubric-based reinforcement learning, in which an LLM judge scores responses against prompt-specific criteria, has become a standard way to post-train language models on tasks without verifiable answers. The rubric, however, is only a proxy for response quality, and a policy trained against it long enough learns to exploit the difference. We train models with GRPO on medical and science rubrics and grade their responses on out-of-distribution (OOD) benchmarks throughout training with two judges: the training judge and a stronger OOD judge. The two scores first rise together and then diverge. The policy is learning to satisfy the training judge without writing better responses, which is a form of reward hacking. To mitigate it, we propose Rubric Dropout, which applies dropout to rubric criteria: at each training step, a random subset of each rubric's criteria is removed from the reward, so the rewarded subset of criteria is resampled at each step. All responses sampled for the same prompt are scored on the same subset, so their rewards remain comparable, and evaluation always uses the full rubric. We also introduce Block Dropout, which drops semantically similar criteria together so that a near duplicate of a dropped criterion cannot keep rewarding the same behavior. Across both benchmarks and models from 1.7B to 8B, dropout improves the gold judge's score on OOD benchmarks by up to 3 percent on HealthBench-Hard and 7 percent on ResearchQA, and it reduces both the gap between the two judges and over-crediting. It keeps in-domain reward close to the baseline, and dropout rates of 30-50 percent work best. Block Dropout improves the gold judge's OOD score further at the same dropout rate. Changing which criteria are rewarded at each step is a simple, inexpensive way to make rubric rewards harder to exploit.

replace-cross AVA-Encoder: Towards Agent-Native Video Representation Learning

Authors: Chuyue Li, Jinpeng Yu, Haozhe Wang, Tian Xueyun, Zhijing Zhang, Bingnan Li, Shuqi Gu, Kan Ren, Jiaming Liu, Ruihua Huang

Abstract: Agentic video creation systems remain far from cinematic-grade quality: their underlying models struggle to plan how story, characters, visuals, and sound fit together, and high-quality creation trajectories to learn such planning abilities are scarce. Finished human films embody this knowledge, but in a tightly coupled audiovisual form that agents cannot directly read or manipulate. We propose the Agentic Video Auto-Encoder (AVA-Encoder), which works backward from finished films, turning them into agent-native representations with reconstruction itself as supervision. It encodes a film into a Film Knowledge Graph (Film KG) of structured-text nodes, typed dependency edges, and linked multimodal assets, then regenerates the film from this graph with a fixed decoder. Reconstruction residuals are distilled into textual gradients that drive dual-loop self-evolution: an outer loop learns a shared encoding policy across videos, and an inner loop refines each video's Film KG at test time. AVA-Encoder improves reconstruction fidelity by 20.7 percentage points over the strongest baseline; its learned policy outperforms a finely human-tuned policy with over 70 percent fewer system-prompt tokens; and Film KGs support coordinated editing and improve all tested downstream creation systems. We release the framework, a reconstruction benchmark, and a Film KG dataset.

replace-cross Mechanistic Circuit Identification for Controllable Data Generation

Authors: Nakyung Lee, Sangwoo Hong, Jungwoo Lee

Abstract: While recent advances in data synthesis aim to curate high-quality datasets, most generation pipelines still rely on heuristic prompt-based control. This black-box paradigm provides limited insight into how individual samples interact with a model's underlying learning dynamics. To bridge this gap, we propose a circuit-grounded framework that connects training-dynamics-based data valuation with mechanistic interpretability (MI). Specifically, we conceptualize data quality along three complementary utility axes, learnability, challenge, and alignment. First, we uncover specialized model-internal circuits that causally govern these utility signals. Then, moving beyond heuristic prompting toward mechanistic control, we leverage these circuits as controllable interfaces, actively steering generation to produce utility-targeted data. Building on this capability, we introduce SAMS (Stage-Aware Mechanistic Scheduling), which schedules circuit-steered data according to the model's evolving optimization needs. Experiments on multiple-choice QA tasks demonstrate that our approach yields precisely controlled data with greater diversity than prompt-based baselines, consistently improving downstream performance and calibration. Ultimately, this work establishes a principled white-box paradigm for interpretable data generation, pioneering the use of MI not just as an analytical tool, but as a practical, controllable interface.

replace-cross The Dice Roll Method: A Standardized Protocol for Repeated-Query Auditing of Large Language Model Brand Recommendations

Authors: Dmitrij \.Zatuchin

Abstract: Background: Researchers increasingly use repeated identical prompts to audit stochastic variation in large language model (LLM) brand recommendations, yet no standardized protocol exists for setting iteration counts, selecting stability metrics, or establishing reliability thresholds. Objective: We formalize the Dice Roll Method as a reusable protocol for repeated-query auditing of LLM brand recommendations, grounded in a generative model of temperature-scaled nucleus sampling. Methods: Total response variance is decomposed into sampling, prompt-phrasing, run-to-run, and model-version components. The stack: a negative-binomial mixed model with iterations as repeated measures; Cliff's delta as the distribution-free effect size; dependence-preserving bootstrap; simulation-based power; a generalizability-theory decomposition; drift diagnostics on pinned snapshots. We reanalyse five brand-recommendation auditing studies: approximately 190,000 observations, 270+ brands, 6 languages, iteration counts 5 to 40. Results: The recomputed D-study supports no fixed tiers. Across 250 distinct questions (S5), G is 0.96 at five runs and 1 to 2 runs reach 0.80, also within each model. For five versions of one question (S1 Valentine's subset, Gemini) it is 0.27 (95% CI 0.00 to 0.52), which fixes no run count. Runs follow from a pilot: n = 4 x residual / cell variance. Simulated single-cell power for a large Cliff's delta is 0.15 to 0.19 at five runs and 0.8 at 15 to 25. The four metric families (count, set, embedding, PASOR) are complementary. A pre-registered external validation on three independent corpora reproduces the D-study prediction in 37 of 39 cells with no failures; fixed tiers do not transfer. Conclusion: The protocol gives repeated-query auditing of LLM brand recommendations a statistically principled footing under the conditional, non-Gaussian structure of real autoregressive generation.

replace-cross A Computational Framework for Modelling Organisation-Level Semantic Identity from Longitudinal Textual Data

Authors: Brinda Murali Krishna, Oktay Karaku\c{s}, Can Ey\"upo\u{g}lu

Abstract: Can longitudinal texts produced by organisationally affiliated contributors reveal collective semantic differentiation and change without obscuring differences among those contributors? We develop a computational social science framework that combines established semantic and graph measures into interpretable organisational fingerprints and temporal profiles. We use \emph{organisation-level semantic identity} to describe a corpus-dependent interpretation of recurring and changing features of affiliated textual output, rather than corporate culture or managerial intention. Drawing on an inherited semantic landscape, we examine 1,289 songs by 126 artists affiliated with four K-pop entertainment companies between 1997 and 2023. The evidence for differentiation is selective. After correction, all three novelty-related measures show artist-level differences, whereas the diversity and connectivity measures tested do not; only two company--community enrichments remain significant. Temporal trajectories reveal further distinctions between portfolios. Artist-grouped balanced accuracy reaches 0.334 against a chance baseline of 0.25, providing modest supporting evidence of affiliation-related signal rather than independent validation of identity. Robustness analyses support the presence of continuous semantic structure more strongly than precise rankings. Together, these findings show how collective regularities can coexist with shared content, contributor heterogeneity and change over time, while leaving cross-domain generality and broader organisational construct validity unresolved.

replace-cross SecJev: Bringing Security Expertise to System One Decision Models

Authors: Zheng Chen, Fei Yu, Haohao Huang, Yang Li, Anlong Chen, Lei Chen

Abstract: Security workflows need models that turn complex observations and explicit policies into decisions. System One models introduced by Jev return typed predictions and probabilities; security specialization supplies the domain expertise behind those predictions. We introduce SecJev, to our knowledge the first family of Jev-style decision models specialized for security, spanning 0.8B to 9B parameters. Built on Kev's single-pass candidate scorer, SecJev learns Boolean, choice, and ordered decisions from text, telemetry, and observation histories. We develop SecJev-Corpus to unify source-label prediction and explicit-policy evaluation across 14 tasks and eight sources. Security specialization improves every model in the family; SecJev-0.8B outperforms general Kev-9B by 20.51 percentage points in task-macro accuracy. At 0.8B and 4B, SecJev achieves accuracy and latency close to answer-only generative fine-tuning with lower peak allocated GPU memory. SecJev-4B improves on Kev-4B and security-trained generation in prompt-injection and traffic transfer tests. We release adapters, decision heads, SecJev-Corpus, and training and inference code.

replace-cross Lost in the bf16 Cast: Exporting Ternary Language Models Can Revert Most Low-Learning-Rate Code Changes

Authors: Avichal Sahai (Ofbusiness), Nishant Raj (Ofbusiness), Animesh Srivastava (Ofbusiness)

Abstract: Ternary language models such as BitNet b1.58, Falcon-E and BitCPM are fine-tuned with higher-precision latent weights and deployed as ternary codes produced by an export step that, in the labs' documented pipelines, first casts the latents to bf16. We audit those pipelines across three labs. In released checkpoints, fp32 quantization of the shipped latents disagrees with the deployed codes on 0.83-1.77% of codes in Falcon-E and BitCPM and on 1.530% in BitNet 2B-4T; for Falcon-E and BitCPM most disagreements are products that bf16 rounding lands exactly on the threshold, which ties-to-even maps to zero, and the unmodified onebitllms exporter reproduces all four Falcon-E releases byte for byte. At fine-tuned endpoints, with learning rates selected to match a nominal learning-rate-to-bf16-ULP ratio, the documented export lowers greedy GSM8K strict accuracy from 58.79% to 0.78% for Falcon-E-1B-Base and from 36.13% to 0.39% for BitCPM-CANN-0.5B, and a bf16 save and reload lowers BitNet 2B-4T's strict accuracy by 27.54 points while its last-number accuracy rises. Two compatibility remedies, writing the training quantizer's codes directly or adjusting the bf16 inputs until the unchanged tools emit them, each met a 4-point strict-accuracy non-inferiority criterion against online evaluation in all three models. In a prospectively frozen study of bf16 latents during fine-tuning (one task, one recipe and trajectory per arm), native bf16 writes left 2B-4T's codes unchanged at the endpoint and changed one Falcon-E code, while emulated stochastic-rounding and compensated writers changed codes and were non-inferior to fp32 training at the same margin in both models. In two model families, randomized interventions on the initial distance from the threshold support distance-dependent selection of the codes that fine-tuning changes.

replace-cross Self-Retrospection Distillation: Turning Post-hoc Experiences into Prior Foresight

Authors: Haoxiang Zhang, Qinglin Chen, Hiroaki Hayashi, Zhuofeng Li, Siming Zhang, Jiaxin Zhang, Jixuan Chen, Fang Wu, Pan Lu, Silvio Savarese, Julian McAuley, Chien-Sheng Wu

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) turns agent experience into learning signals primarily through scalar outcome rewards after interaction. For group-relative objectives, however, this signal vanishes when all rollouts receive the same reward, even though their trajectories may reveal useful information about what the task requires and how the agent fails. We ask a complementary question: can hindsight teach an agent what it could have anticipated before acting? We introduce prospective learning, which uses post-hoc experience to supervise foresight predictions from the pre-interaction view, and instantiate it with Self-Retrospection Distillation (SRD). Intuitively, a completed trajectory reveals knowledge that would have been useful and pitfalls that should be avoided; SRD distills this privileged hindsight into trajectory-blind foresight of the same policy. Foresight serves only as a training target and need not be explicitly generated at inference time. Across 10 tool-integrated reasoning and long-horizon agentic tasks, SRD complements RLVR and self-distillation baselines with gains of up to 24.2 pp. Its advantage is especially pronounced when reward contrast is scarce: when 37--98% of rollout groups are reward-uniform across model scales, yet SRD can still exploit learning signal from sampled trajectories. In the 2B setting, where 98% of groups are all-failure, the RLVR training ends up at 0.0% success, while adding SRD reaches 60.6% under the same rollout budget. Our results suggest that post-hoc agent experience is useful not only for evaluating or improving behavior, but also for shaping predictive representations before available interaction. Training code can be found at https://github.com/SalesforceAIResearch/SRD.

URLs: https://github.com/SalesforceAIResearch/SRD.

replace-cross Do LLMs Learn from Rewards in Context? : Rethinking the role of reward in In-Context RL

Authors: Minchan Kwon, Seunghee Koh, Sunghyun Baek, Minsung Bae, Junmo Kim

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly improve at inference time by accumulating experience in context rather than by updating parameters. This process is often described as in-context reinforcement learning (ICRL). Whether in-context learning (ICL) can actually play the role of RL, however, has not been tested. We study this question in its simplest form, direct ICRL, where the model conditions directly on raw trajectory-reward pairs, and ask whether the reward acts as a learning signal. Through controlled experiments on four benchmarks across six models, we find that the reward is read, but its effect is small: flipping, randomizing, or removing the reward leaves the improvement curve almost unchanged, and this holds even under meta-prompts that explicitly instruct the model to explore, exploit, or reason over rewards. Trajectories drive improvement, but not through their semantic content: shuffled or corrupted trajectories work as well as real ones. These patterns closely mirror those known in ICL, suggesting that direct ICRL is better understood as a special case of ICL than as inference-time RL. This reframing has implications for agent memory design: ICL factors such as input distribution and demonstrations may matter more than RL elements such as reward shaping and exploration.

replace-cross RealityBench: Simulated Environments to Evaluate a Vision Language Model's Ability to Document Human Physical Work

Authors: Andrew Perrault (Relantic, Inc., Columbus, OH, USA), Anmol Takiar (Relantic, Inc., Columbus, OH, USA), Dominik Winecki (Relantic, Inc., Columbus, OH, USA), Arnab Nandi (Relantic, Inc., Columbus, OH, USA)

Abstract: Augmenting worker capabilities in the physical world through guidance has been a decades-long goal for AI agents. One key supporting capability is to understand what the worker is doing, seeing through the human's camera or smart glasses, and assist them in documentation. Despite advances in vision language models, their capabilities in these settings remain uncertain and are challenging to benchmark because most physical work data is proprietary, and involves too much variability to study in a principled manner. Unlike structured settings such as kitchens and warehouses that have predictable lighting, geometry, and objects, field-facing tasks such as rooftop solar installation and vehicle repair are unconstrained and high variance. Here, we model real-world physical work in Blender across eleven environments, incorporating variants in both image acceptability and documentation. The acceptability task asks whether a particular view would be suitable proof-of-work and documentation asks whether an agent can understand the task-relevant state of the environment from that view. Across 26 vision-language models, we find that top models largely solve documentation when the answer is text in frame, but are weak at reading measurements and at judging small parts and spatial relations, such as whether a tape measure leans. Acceptability is harder to pin down because human raters disagree with each other. Measured against a scorer that agrees with the raters' majority about as often as a single rater does, the best models cannot be distinguished from a single non-author rater, at a cost within the range of a human pass. In the three environments where we can trace acceptability errors, most wrong rejections come from misreading the scene rather than the requirement. On the three documentation questions for which we have real images, model rankings on rendered frames transfer to real images.

replace-cross How to Build a Benchmark That Actually Measures Taste

Authors: Xiaofan Wu, Xi Zeng, Miaoxia Chen, Peishan Chen, Shuyan Li, Chen Wang, Xiaohan Wang, Wanling Xu, Jiyun Yao, Hanyong Zhong, Jiahao Zhu

Abstract: Expert-aligned "taste" benchmarks should reward craft judgment, not the ability to game the test. We introduce TasteBench, a pairwise good-vs-bad benchmark across visual, brand, book, and tech items under a frozen rubric with expert-only gold (panel n>=3; percent agreement >=0.60 primary gate; weak engagement labels train/analysis-only), together with a control protocol that separates craft judgment from test-gaming. Applying the protocol to a Round-1 pilot (primary_human_expert; three biological human experts; n=250 pairs) surfaces and quantifies three validity threats: two construction artifacts that inflate the trivial-baseline layer, and a difficulty ceiling that inflates every score. (1) Position: the preferred item is slot A with probability 0.956, so an always_item_a policy scores 0.956 (fair chance 0.5). (2) Lexical leakage: a filename token cue scores 0.912. (3) Difficulty ceiling: craft domains show near-ceiling human agreement (book/brand/tech 1.0), and even under A/B randomization (eval_assignment_seed7; gold_side_A ~0.47) all five VLM cards reach 1.0 on craft, with only visual separating (~0.84-0.91; a post-hoc hard subset, n=107, leaves craft at 1.0). The protocol (expert-only gold, a weak-label firewall, side randomization at construction, filename scrubbing, and hard filters) makes each artifact measurable and, for the two construction artifacts, correctable; we release a reference eval harness and a side-randomization tool, and show the tool removes the position skew (0.916 to 0.495/0.505). The result is a calibrated view of what the benchmark does and does not yet measure: position and leakage are fixable, whereas the craft-domain ceiling is a pair-difficulty limit that Round-2 must address. We do not evaluate engagement, era/circle conditioning, or calibration here, and we claim no general taste SOTA.

replace-cross AlphaRLM: Aligning Language Models for Literature-Grounded Symbolic Factor Discovery

Authors: Jie Huang, Yuecheng Hong, Yao Chen, Hao Zhong, Linfeng Xiao, Xudong Liu, Qiang Ye, Hao Xia, Jianxuan Xin

Abstract: Formulaic alpha mining seeks to discover executable expressions that capture predictive market signals in a vast, noisy search space. Recent LLM-based approaches use prompting, iterative search, or seed-factor evolution, leaving direct factor construction through policy learning less explored. To address this, we introduce AlphaRLM, a reinforcement-learning framework that trains LLMs to construct executable symbolic factors from financial literature without input factor expressions. AlphaRLM incorporates Literature-Guided Operator Assembly (LEGO), which conditions factor construction on literature-derived ideas and executable operators, providing semantic priors for exploration. It further aligns the generation policy with market feedback through executable-validity and grouped-return-separation rewards that capture non-monotonic structures across factor quantiles. Extensive experiments on CSI 300, CSI 1000, and S&P 500 show that AlphaRLM outperforms strong factor-mining and LLM-based baselines in annualized excess return. Notably, the 8B model achieves strong cross-market performance, highlighting the promise of literature-conditioned, market-aligned factor generation.

replace-cross Hidden in Plain Sight: Advancing Harmful Content Understanding via Perception-Aware Adversarial Self-Play

Authors: Yisong Xiao, Benlei Cui, Haiwen Hong, Shiji Zhao, Zonghao Ying, Yaoyuan Zhang, Longtao Huang, Aishan Liu, Hui Xue, Ting Ma, Xianglong Liu

Abstract: Despite significant advances in language understanding, Large Language Models (LLMs) can still miss meaning hidden in plain sight, failing to identify harmful intent in expressions readily understood by humans. Malicious users exploit this gap by strategically disguising harmful content to evade automated moderation, posing a serious threat to online platform safety. The vulnerability reflects a fundamental cognitive asymmetry between human readers, who integrate contextual, phonetic, and visual cues to decipher the intent behind deliberate obfuscation, and text-based LLMs, which rely primarily on token sequences and have limited exposure to evolving evasions. To bridge this asymmetry, we propose PASP (Perception-Aware Adversarial Self-Play), which combines multi-perceptual views with adversarial self-play to improve understanding of harmful intent as evasions evolve. Specifically, PASP constructs textual, phonetic, and visual views of each input, preserving its original context while exposing pronunciation and glyph cues obscured in token space. During adversarial self-play, an attacker uses defender feedback to generate intent-preserving evasions, while the defender is trained using an intent-invariance reward to understand harmful intent across changing surface forms. Extensive experiments show that PASP reduces adaptive attack success rates by up to 73.71% and 74.49% on ChineseHarm-Bench and EvoHarmBench, respectively, preserves model capabilities with minimal degradation, and effectively interprets real-world evasive expressions in e-commerce.

replace-cross Who Sets the Pace of Judge Hacking? Judge Capability versus the Policy-Judge Gap

Authors: Haosen Wang

Abstract: Under GRPO against a frozen, reference-free LLM judge, RL exploits the judge's false positives. Does the policy-judge capability gap or the judge itself set how fast and how far? We train 0.5B and 1.5B Qwen2.5 policies on GSM8K against four judges at least as large, and the 1.5B policy also against one smaller judge. In a post-hoc analysis, judge acceptance rose, but no cell of 12 gained more than 0.04 in seed-mean held-out greedy accuracy. A gold-reward control with the same recipe raised the 1.5B policy's accuracy by +0.054 (for the 0.5B policy, gold reward gave no detectable gain either). The depth of judge hacking tracks the judge: the plateau correlates with judge AUC (Spearman -0.69 over 12 cells), and within a family the larger judge is fooled less deeply. For the pace, two pre-specified pairs contradict the gap account: the cell with the larger gap was fooled 3.5x sooner at the pre-specified threshold. With 12 cells, however, we cannot formally separate judge AUC from the gap for either endpoint. A blind test of step-0 predictors on three held-out judges was inconclusive. At 7-8B, judges from three families plateaued between 0.37 and 0.85.

replace-cross REVEAL: Reverse Engineering Agentic Latents from Frontier-Model Traces

Authors: Yuetian Du, Meng Wang, Yucheng Wang, Zhangzhe Chen, Wei Wang, Boyu Yang, Weiqi Zhai, Bing Zhao, Ming Kong, Zhan Zhou, Jie Liu, Qiang Zhu

Abstract: Frontier LLM agents perform complex interactive tasks, but their inference costs motivate transferring their experience to smaller open-source agents. Distillation and latent communication typically require target-model training or access to source-model internals. We study how textual frontier-model trajectories can provide reusable local controls for a frozen agent. Inspired by protocol reverse engineering, we introduce $\textit{Agentic Latents}$, comprising anchored states and dynamic profiles of feedback--response cycles elicited by replay in the target model. Our training-free framework, $\textbf{REVEAL}$ ($\textbf{REV}$erse $\textbf{E}$ngineering $\textbf{A}$gentic $\textbf{L}$atents), uses these profiles and trajectory outcomes to match and select local events from successful and failed rollouts of the same task and source model. Their response-transition contrasts yield a target-specific steering direction, applied before each response. Within-benchmark evaluations on Terminal-Bench 2.1 and Humanity's Last Exam with tools report higher accuracy than unmodified agents across three targets. Representation analyses and behavioral case studies support reusing frontier-model experience as local activation control.

replace-cross RELAY: Selective Self-Distillation with Adaptive Hand-off for Rubric-Based Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Kunwang Zheng, Xinyu Sun, Yu Cao, Shuocheng Li, Lei Cheng, Ying Liu, Qilong Zhang, Jinlong Chen, Hua Yang, Qianglong Chen

Abstract: Rubric-based reinforcement learning grades a response against many written criteria but collapses them into one reward, which discards the detail the rubric carries and gives no GRPO reward gradient when a group's responses tie. A frozen teacher that reads the rubric as privileged context provides token-level supervision, but we observe an early plateau in the gains over GRPO. RELAY uses advantages and rewards to select positive-advantage and low-scoring zero-advantage rollouts, and monitors the distillation loss to decide when to retire the teacher. Stage 1 combines selective distillation from a frozen teacher with GRPO; a plateau detector then removes the teacher, and Stage 2 continues with GRPO alone. Calibrated on one model-domain pair and reused unchanged in three others, the plateau detector fires between steps 122 and 171. RELAY scores highest in-domain among the compared methods in all four settings, ahead of GRPO on RaR-Medicine by 4.62 points with Qwen3-1.7B and 6.02 with Qwen3-4B. On Qwen3-1.7B/Medicine, removing the teacher from a shared checkpoint outperforms keeping, weakening or refreshing it. Distilling selected rollouts covering about half the response tokens also outperforms distilling all rollouts at a matched average coefficient.

replace-cross SkillScreen: High-Recall Screening of Malicious Agent Skills with Decision Models

Authors: Jerrold Huang, Junwei Quan, Saiyue Lyu, Zihan Guo, Yingqiang Ge

Abstract: Agent skills turn natural-language instructions into actions, creating a security challenge: legitimate automation and malicious behaviour can look deceptively similar. A practical screen must catch nearly every attack while keeping the review load manageable. We present SkillScreen, a compact decision model trained to score skill maliciousness directly from SKILL$.$md in a single forward pass. On MaliciousSkillBench, SkillScreen flags 20-25% of benign skills at 99% recall on unseen attack families, substantially fewer than the word-level classifier and prompted LLM judges tested. Under the same training protocol, it also generalizes better than a fully fine-tuned encoder to unseen sources. Yet unfamiliar benign sources sharply increase false positives, showing that recognizing new attacks does not ensure reliable screening of new skill collections. Our results demonstrate the value of task-trained decision models for high-recall skill screening. Project page: https://jerroldh.github.io/SkillScreen-webpage/

URLs: https://jerroldh.github.io/SkillScreen-webpage/

replace-cross Fish Audio Drama 3 Technical Report

Authors: Dawei Han, Shidong Li, Shijia Liao, Jiahua Liu, Songting Liu, Qingzheng Wang, Yuxuan Wang, Xin Chen, Yifan Cheng, Tianyu Li, Gangin Park, Xuehao Yang, Chang Zeng, Haoyang Zhang, Ruoyi Zhang, Xiangyu Zhang

Abstract: We introduce Fish Audio Drama 3, an open-source speech generation model that turns text into directed performances, built on a 35B-parameter mixture-of-experts backbone that activates 3B parameters per token. It clones voices from multiple references, follows natural-language direction, revises a take when the next turn gives a note, voices multi-character scenes, edits existing speech by infilling, and synthesizes long-form and streaming audio. Multi-turn revision, scene dubbing, editing by infilling, and streaming text input are new in Drama 3, all in one model. Drama 3 pairs the hybrid linear-attention backbone with a new codebook multi-token-prediction audio decoder that predicts the acoustic codebooks of each frame in dedicated layers that attend over the entire sequence. The model is trained with a three-stage pre-training recipe, instruction fine-tuning, and large-batch reinforcement learning whose only reward is a single learned model of human preference. Its inference engine decodes a single stream at 271 frames per second on one H200, against 177 for Fish Audio S2, although Drama 3 has more than seven times as many parameters. The model weights are available on GitHub (https://github.com/fishaudio/fish-speech) and Hugging Face (https://huggingface.co/fishaudio/drama-3), and inference is available through SGLang-Omni (https://github.com/sgl-project/sglang-omni). We highly encourage readers to visit https://fish.audio to direct their own performances.

URLs: https://github.com/fishaudio/fish-speech), https://huggingface.co/fishaudio/drama-3),, https://github.com/sgl-project/sglang-omni)., https://fish.audio

replace-cross Readout Scaling Shapes Sparse Feature Recovery

Authors: Brian Xiao, Sarah Wilson

Abstract: Readout scaling changes what a sparse autoencoder (SAE) learns, but the benefit of removing it depends on the data. We use readout-canonicalized decision-space (RC-DS) coordinates to remove singular-value gains while preserving retained decision information. At a fixed readout condition number, changing the generating distribution reverses which training coordinates recover known factors best. In our controlled coordinate-matching study, recovery is highest when training restores the space in which the factors were generated. When factors are generated before readout scaling, RC-DS increases mean sparse-factor recovery from 0.506 to 0.913 at condition number 100, compared with centered-logit training under the same budget. An exact wrapper reproduces RC-DS training to numerical precision, isolating training geometry rather than additional information. In neural policies, RC-DS improves mean logit reconstruction under the tested budgets. On three fixed language-model checkpoints, RC-DS has higher mean gain-free reconstruction in all 18 tested task-sparsity settings, while centered-logit SAEs reconstruct output logits better in 17. Small-label-head controls show that longer training reduces the reconstruction gap from 0.0198 to 0.0016. Together, these results show how readout scaling, the data distribution, and training shape sparse learning. RC-DS provides an information-preserving method for studying and controlling the readout's contribution. Separate label-detection and steering tests distinguish these reconstruction effects from task-specific feature usefulness.

replace-cross Does Pretraining Teach Chemical Language Models Chemistry?

Authors: Leif Sieben, Yoel Zimmermann

Abstract: Chemical language models (CLMs) are pretrained on large corpora of molecules with the aim of learning representations that transfer to diverse downstream tasks. Here, we systematically study the effects of pretraining using a series of such CLMs across 64 molecular property prediction datasets. We find that the downstream gains of pretraining in CLMs do not scale and performance saturates below gradient-boosting baselines. Across the regimes evaluated, only supervised pretraining on traditional physico-chemical descriptors consistently improves downstream performance for the CLMs tested whereas some forms of pretraining result in models worse than a randomly initialized encoder. While models can resolve molecular structure well, they do so no better than a randomly initialized model, suggesting that, at best, CLMs learn descriptor-like properties in addition to low-level token statistics during pretraining, but surprisingly little chemistry beyond what classical molecular features already capture.

replace-cross Pack Light, CarryOn: Building Object-Oriented Context for Long-Horizon Agents

Authors: Yuchen Lu, Julian McAuley, Lei Zhang

Abstract: Long-horizon agents accumulate progress in persistent task state, yet repeatedly process interaction histories describing how that progress was achieved. This raises a natural question: can long-horizon context instead be organized around reusable progress rather than the history that produced it? In this paper, we introduce CarryOn, an object-oriented context runtime that organizes continuation around reusable task objects. Rather than treating compressed histories or reconstructed accounts of progress as the primary basis for continuation, CarryOn builds a context view from continuation carriers, complementary execution context, and a live tail of recent interactions. A core challenge is keeping this reusable basis valid after its surrounding history is retired: how can CarryOn update the basis without reconstructing the full representation at every fold? CarryOn maintains carrier records across folds, revising affected records against bound evidence and applicable environment checks while carrying forward unaffected records and jointly updating execution context. Experiments on OSWorld 2.0 and BrowseComp-Plus show improved gains of 5.8% and 7.0% while reducing total token usage by 51.9% and 22.9%, respectively. Project page: https://ethannju.github.io/carryon

URLs: https://ethannju.github.io/carryon

replace-cross Read the Room Before Your Model Generates

Authors: Muyao Kong, Zhihao Lin

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) generally rely on sampling temperature to regulate the amount of randomness in their outputs. Mainstream methods for selecting temperature typically involve fixed, heuristic global settings and context or token-level calibration, rather than a geometrically interpretable representation of the communicative scenario. In this work, we introduce a simple and interpretable temperature selection framework, the Scenario-Actor, which adapts decoding to the inferred situational characteristics. Scenario-Actor extracts scenario-relevant structures from Transformer hidden states and determines the temperature from scenario embedding, a structured geometric representation of the communicative setting. Empirical results show that on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Scenario-Actor achieves strong goal-completion performance in interactive social scenarios, significant gains on open-ended instruction following, and competitive performance on multi-turn conversation compared to multiple baselines.
